MADHO LAL HUSSAIN (LAHORE) — consolidated shrine entry (long / holistic)
Sheet row: "Shrine of Hazrat Madho Lal Hussain (Shah Hussain Darbar)" / Location: Baghbanpura (near Shalimar Gardens), Lahore
Sources: 4 books + your field survey (listed in the Bibliography). Copy the block from "## Overview"
down to the end of "## Legacy" into the sheet's "Description" column.
=====================================================================================

## Overview

In Baghbanpura, close by the Shalimar Gardens on the eastern edge of Lahore, stands the shrine of Hazrat Madho Lal Hussain — the joint name of two men, and the sign of one of the most extraordinary bonds in the spiritual history of the Punjab. Within it rest Shah Hussain (1538–1599), the great Malamati Sufi and father of the Punjabi _kafi_, and Madho Lal, the Hindu youth he loved and whose name he joined for ever to his own. Around the darbar has grown one of the largest festivals in the region, the Mela Chiraghan or “Festival of Lamps,” and from it has flowed a body of Punjabi mystical verse that ordinary people still sing four centuries on. The shrine is at once a place of ecstatic devotion, a monument of Punjabi language and poetry, and a beloved emblem of love that overleaps the boundaries of caste and creed. To understand it, one must begin with the strange and luminous life of Shah Hussain.

## The Life of Shah Hussain

Hussain was born in Lahore in 1538 into a family of weavers — his father, by tradition, a craftsman who stitched uniforms for the army of the Emperor Humayun. He was a child of unusual gifts and piety: he is said to have committed the Qur’an to memory by the age of seven, and by his mid-teens to have begun teaching recitation and preaching, leading the congregational prayers and the long Taraweeh prayers of Ramadan. For some twenty years he lived the life of an exact and disciplined scholar of the outward law, his devotion so evident that learned men sought his company and came away loving him. In his spiritual training he is remembered as a disciple of the Sufi master Bahlol (Bahlol Daryai) and as a devoted frequenter of the shrine of Data Ganj Bakhsh; there, the accounts say, he withdrew into a long spiritual retreat, keeping _chilla_ by the darbar of Lahore’s patron saint for some twelve years, absorbed in the discipline of divine love (_ishq-e-haqiqi_), while those who came to sit with him took to calling him, simply, “Baba.”

Then, around the age of thirty-two, everything changed. The scrupulous scholar became a _malang_ — a wandering, dishevelled ecstatic. He set aside the robes and the reputation of a preacher, and took up instead the crimson dress by which he is remembered as “Lal” (the Red) Hussain, dancing the _dhamal_ and carrying a flask in his hand that the scandalised took for wine. What had happened, in the language of the Sufis, was that the outward path of the _Shariat_ had opened, for Hussain, onto the burning inward path of love — and he chose to walk it in the most confronting way he could.

## The Way of Blame

Hussain belonged, in spirit, to the _Malamatiyya_ — the Sufis of the “path of blame,” who deliberately drew the censure of respectable society upon themselves in order to kill the ego’s hunger for esteem, hiding their inner sanctity beneath an outward show of impropriety. To dance and sing and carry a flask through the streets of Mughal Lahore was, for such a mystic, not a fall from grace but a discipline of self-effacement, a refusal of the world’s praise. Inevitably it drew fatwas and denunciations, and at last complaints reached the imperial court: some demanded that this man who seemed to mock the outward forms of religion be put to death.

The most famous story of his life belongs to this moment. Summoned before the ruler — the accounts name the Emperor Jahangir — and challenged over the wine he was said to carry, Hussain is said to have poured out his flask into the cups set before the court: and the liquid ran, in the first cup, as milk; in the second, as honey; and in the third, as the sacred water of Zamzam. The king and his courtiers, astonished, understood that they stood before no ordinary man. “Let him be,” they said; “he belongs to another world — let no one trouble him after this day.” The saint went his way, and the tale has been told ever since as a sign that what the world condemns in the friends of God it does not understand.

## Madho Lal: A Love Made Legend

The turning-point of Hussain’s later life, and the reason his name is joined to another’s, was his love for Madho Lal — a young man of a Hindu Brahmin family. The saint, it is said, was overcome at the sight of the boy’s beauty; when people objected that a Muslim faqir should be so taken with a Hindu youth, Hussain answered that his love was not carnal but a kind of wonder: “I do not love him — I envy his Creator, for how beautiful He has made him.” Slowly the two became inseparable, though Madho’s family and caste-fellows looked on the friendship with disapproval. Devotional tradition preserves a famous miracle of their bond: when Madho was bound by custom to make the pilgrimage of the Ganga Snan (the ritual bathing in the Ganges) and feared his parents’ anger, Hussain bade him take his hands, set his feet upon the saint’s own, and close his eyes — and in that instant Madho found himself at the distant bathing-place, to the astonishment of all. From then, the love was mutual and complete: the two could not bear to be parted.

So closely were their names woven together that Hussain gave the youth his own, telling him — when Madho worried that the world knew only “Lal Hussain” — that from that day people would speak Madho’s name before his own. And so it has been: the saint is remembered not as Shah Hussain alone but as _Madho Lal Hussain_, and the two lie buried side by side. In a land later torn by the divisions of religion, the shrine of a Muslim saint and his Hindu beloved, honoured under a single name, has become one of the Punjab’s most cherished symbols of a love that knows nothing of the frontiers of caste and creed — and, for the Sufi, a living parable of the soul’s love for God, in which the lover and the beloved at last become one.

## The Kafis

Shah Hussain is honoured as the first great master of the Punjabi _kafi_ — the short, sung mystical lyric that would become the very heart of Punjabi Sufi poetry, taken up after him by Bulleh Shah, Sultan Bahu, and Khwaja Farid. He is said not to have written his verses down himself; a court official, Dehan Bahar Khan, is remembered as having set them to paper as the saint sang them in his ecstasy. The kafis are brief and burning, cast in the voice of a woman — a bride, a spinner at her wheel, a girl longing for her absent love — for whom the Beloved is God, and separation from Him the only real grief. Almost all of them close with the poet naming himself in the third person, in a signature that has become famous in its own right: _Kahe Hussain, faqir Sain da_ (“Says Hussain, the humble faqir of the Lord”).

In his best-loved kafi the soul cries out in the voice of a daughter to her mother:

مائے نی میں کینوں آکھاں، دردِ وچھوڑے دا حال نی

[“O mother, to whom shall I tell the anguish of this separation?”]

In another, the whole of creation dissolves into the presence of the one Beloved:

رَبّا میرے حال دا محرم توں
اندر توں ہیں، باہر توں ہیں، رومَ رومَ وِچ توں

[“O Lord, You alone are the confidant of my condition — You are within me, You are without me, You are in my every pore.”]

And in another the ache of longing lengthens the very hours of the night:

سجّݨ بِن راتاں ہوئیاں وڈّیاں

[“Without the beloved, the nights have grown long.”]

And in a line that has passed into proverb, he sings of the truth that will not be silenced in the heart of the lover:

منہ آئی بات نہ رہندی اے

[“The word that has risen to the lips cannot be held back.”]

These songs, deceptively simple and endlessly deep, are still sung by qawwals and folk-singers across the Punjab, and it is largely through them that Shah Hussain lives — the man who abandoned the scholar’s certainties for the ecstatic’s, and made of the Punjabi language a vessel for the love of God.

## The Poet and His Age

Shah Hussain lived through the high noon of the Mughal Empire, in the reigns of Akbar and Jahangir, when Lahore was a great imperial capital adorned with gardens, mosques, and mansions. His was an age of ferment — of Akbar’s experiments in the reconciliation of religions and of popular unrest against the imperial order — and Punjabi tradition links the ecstatic faqir with the rebel folk-hero Dulla Bhatti, who rose against the Emperor’s revenue demands, casting Hussain among the friends of the poor and the defiant of the mighty. Among the learned and the holy of the city he had many companions and interlocutors, yet it is the texture of ordinary Punjabi life that fills his verse: the girl at her spinning-wheel (_charkha_), the bride adorned and waiting, the traveller on the long road, the lanes of the parental home that a daughter must one day leave. Into these homely images he poured the whole drama of the soul’s love for God and its grief at separation, so that the humblest villager and the deepest mystic could each find themselves in his song.

In choosing to sing in Punjabi rather than the Persian of the court, and in the voice of a woman rather than that of the scholar, Hussain did something momentous: he made the mother-tongue of the Punjab a vehicle of the highest spiritual expression, and founded the tradition of the mystical _kafi_ that Bulleh Shah, Sultan Bahu, Shah Sharaf, and Khwaja Farid would afterward carry to its heights. His Malamati freedom was itself a kind of statement — a rebuke, danced and sung in the red robe of the malang, to a religion of mere respectability and outward form. In his own person he fused the ecstatic and the poet, the lover and the critic, and left the Punjab a way of speaking to God that it has never since forgotten.

## Death and the Miracle of the Grave

Shah Hussain died in 1599. Foreseeing his end, the accounts say, he had sent Madho away — some say he had him enrolled in the army — knowing that his beloved could not bear to watch him die; and he left a strange instruction, that he be buried first on the very bank of the river Ravi, where the flood would in time carry the grave away. When the waters shifted the grave, he said, they should dig at the place where it came to rest, and if a rosary (_tasbih_), a water-pot (_lota_), and a prayer-mat (_jaanamaz_) were found in the earth, they would know they had found the true spot. He was buried by the Ravi, and Madho, told of his death only afterward, abandoned everything and returned, never again to leave the grave. Some years later the river flooded, as the saint had foretold; the grave was carried to Baghbanpura, where a _jogi_ (a Hindu ascetic) dwelt, and there, when the ground was opened, the rosary, the pot, and the prayer-mat were found exactly as Hussain had promised. On that spot Madho reburied him, and there he kept his long vigil, reciting his master’s kafis in the streets and at the tomb, until, decades later, he too died and was laid to rest beside the friend whose name he bore — as had been Hussain’s last wish.

## The Shrine

The darbar stands in Baghbanpura, in the shadow of the Mughal Shalimar Gardens, and spreads today over some twenty kanals of ground. At its heart lie the two graves — Shah Hussain’s and, beside it, Madho Lal’s — the object of a constant stream of pilgrims who come to recite the Fatiha, to make vows, and to sit in the presence of the saint of love. A mosque adjoins the shrine, where the five daily prayers are held, and around the complex has grown a large graveyard, for to be buried near Madho Lal Hussain is counted a great blessing, and space within it is hard to come by. The shrine took shape over the centuries after the saint’s death, was substantially rebuilt and enlarged in the nineteenth century, and was most recently renovated by the Punjab Auqaf Department, which administers it, in 2011. Every Thursday evening the tomb is lit with lamps and fills with visitors; on ordinary days, too, some eight to ten thousand people are said to pass through.

## Mela Chiraghan: The Festival of Lamps

The shrine’s crowning event is the Mela Chiraghan — the “Festival of Lamps” — the annual urs held in the spring, in the months of March and April, and reckoned among the greatest festivals of the Punjab. For three days the darbar and its surroundings blaze with countless lamps and candles, and the crowds swell from the daily thousands to well over a hundred thousand, drawn from far and wide. Each day carries its own character: one given to the fair itself, with _dhamal_ danced to the beat of the _dhol_; one to the lighting of the lamps (_chiraghan_) from which the festival takes its name; and one to the ceremonial changing of the _chadar_, the cloth spread over the grave — the whole accompanied by qawwali, by _langar_ (the free distribution of food), and by great gatherings of devotees. The festival has ancient associations with the neighbouring Shalimar Gardens, and it belongs as much to the shared popular culture of Lahore as to the shrine alone; for generations it has been one of the city’s defining occasions, a meeting of the sacred and the festive in which the memory of the poet-saint is renewed in light, music, and dance.

Historically the Mela Chiraghan was among the very largest fairs of the Punjab — at its height, it is said, rivalling even the great spring festival of Basant — and it was long celebrated in and around the Shalimar Gardens themselves, the lamps spilling out from the shrine into the royal garden. Under Sikh rule in the nineteenth century Maharaja Ranjit Singh and his court are remembered as joining in the festivities, a mark of how far the saint’s appeal reached beyond the Muslim community. In time the fair was drawn back within the shrine and its precincts, but it remains a vast and joyous occasion, binding the memory of the poet-saint to the civic life of Lahore and gathering Muslims and non-Muslims, the devout and the merely festive, into the same three nights of light.

## Devotion and Significance

The devotion at Madho Lal Hussain draws on everything the saint was: the ecstatic malang who danced where others prayed; the poet whose kafis put the love of God into the language of the people; and the lover whose bond with a Hindu youth became a byword for a love beyond division. Pilgrims come for the blessing of a saint famous for _ishq-e-haqiqi_, the “real love” of the Divine, and for the sense — as the surveyors of the shrine record the local belief — that Hussain “belonged to another world,” that his whole life was unlike that of ordinary men. Those who could not know him in the flesh, it is said, come to love him through his stories and his songs. His verse is sung at the shrine and across the Punjab; his festival is one of the largest in the land; and his joint name stands, in a country marked by communal division, as a quiet insistence that the highest sanctity and the deepest love recognise no barrier of caste or creed. In this the darbar is not only the tomb of a great saint but a living argument about what devotion, and what love, can mean.

## Legacy

Shah Hussain — Madho Lal Hussain — occupies a place all his own in the memory of the Punjab. He is honoured as the father of the Punjabi kafi and so as the fountainhead of a tradition of Sufi song that runs through Bulleh Shah and Sultan Bahu down to the present day; as a Malamati saint who dramatised, in his own scandalous freedom, the truth that the love of God is worth more than the good opinion of the world; and as the lover of Madho, whose story has made his shrine a symbol of harmony between Muslim and Hindu, and of a love that death itself could not part. Amid the crowds and the lamps of the Mela Chiraghan, and in every kafi sung in his voice, the poet-saint of Lahore lives on.

## Bibliography

- Shafqat Tanvir Mirza, _Shah Hussain_ (Islamabad: Lok Virsa) — Urdu study of the saint’s life, teachers (Bahlol, Data’s darbar), contemporaries, and travels.
- _Kalam-e-Shah Hussain_ (ed. Syed Babar Ali) — a collected edition of Shah Hussain’s Punjabi kafis.
- _Shah Husain_ (Punjabi/Urdu study; from the project’s source collection).
- _Darbar Madho Lal Hussain_ — Urdu account of the shrine and its history.
- Shrines Project field survey, Darbar Madho Lal Hussain responses (surveyor: Saifullah), 2026.

## From Mufti Ghulam Sarwar Lahori, Hadiqat al-Awliya

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/hadeeqat_ul_aulia.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**What the row gains — and the structural problem it raises.** The archive holds one row. **The book
holds two saints, in two separately numbered entries, with two appendix rows and two index lines**,
and never uses the compound name "Madho Lal Hussain" except inside an index parenthesis and in the
title of the editor's own encyclopaedia article. That shape is set out first below, because the
row's shape is the thing in question. The row also gains: Shah Husain's Malamati affiliation and the
reason for "Lal"; his weaver family and Hindu great-grandfather; a quantified following; a named
Persian composition; the editor's argued rejection of the book's death year; Madho's Brahmin
parentage at Shahdara, his conversion, his tenure as *mujawir* at Husain's grave, and his burial
inside his shaikh's enclosure — which is the book's only warrant for a joint shrine — together with
the editor's doubt of Madho's very historicity.

**The two-saint structure, as the book prints it**
- **Shaikh Husain**: "شیخ حسین لاہوری", entry **no. 17**, printed p. 42 (contents, p. 15, folio 3);
  entry at pp. 54–57, folios 42–45, headed "شیخ حسین المشہور بہ لال حسین لاہوری". Born
  "سال نوسو پینتالیس" = **945**, died **1008 AH** in Shah Akbar's reign (pp. 55–56, folios 43–44).
- **Shaikh Madho**: "شیخ مادھو لاہوری", entry **no. 24**, printed p. 52 (contents, p. 15, folio 3);
  entry at pp. 64–65, folios 52–53. Born "نوسو تراسی" with ۹۸۳ in the margin, died
  "ایک ہزار چھپن" with ۱۰۵۶ in the margin, age "تہتر" with ۷۳ in the margin (p. 65, folio 53); the
  editor sharpens the death to **22 Zi al-Hijja 1056 AH / 1646 CE** (Haqiqat al-Fuqara p. 165)
  (p. 65, folio 53, حاشیہ 2).
- **Two appendix rows** (p. 287, folio 275): row 5, "شیخ حسین المشہور بہ لال حسین لاہوری | لاہور |
  مادھو لال حسین سٹریٹ باغبانپورہ"; row 11, "شیخ مادھو قدس سرہ العزیز | | مادھو لال حسین سٹریٹ
  باغبانپورہ" — Madho gets a row of his own, with the honorific "قدس سرہ العزیز" and the title
  "شیخ", and the book's address column for him is **blank** where Husain's reads simply "لاہور".
  Both present locations are the one named street in Baghbanpura.
- **Two index lines.** "حسین لاہوری شاہ (مادھو لال حسین) | ۴۲ ، ۴۵ ، ۵۲" (p. 317, folio 305); est.
  pp. 54, 57, 64. And "مادھو میاں شیخ | ۴۳ ، ۵۲ ، ۵۳" (p. 327, folio 315); est. pp. 55, 64, 65.
  Madho's own line carries **no epithet tying him to Shah Husain and no gloss**; of his three
  folios one (52) is shared with Shah Husain and two (43, 53) are not. The compound "مادھو لال"
  appears only inside the parenthesis of Shah Husain's line.
- **The book's own link between them** is a footnote in Husain's entry — "رجوع کنید بہ احوال مادھو
  کتاب ہذا", see Madho's account in this book (p. 55, folio 43, حاشیہ 1) — and the naming of
  "پہلے دیوان حضرت کے معشوق و محبوب شیخ مادھو", the first Diwan being Shaikh Madho, the Hazrat's
  beloved (p. 55, folio 43). The contents list alone did not supply it (chunk 001), and chunk 026's
  two mentions of the saint carry **no "مادھو" and no "شاہ حسین"** at all (pp. 271, 273).
- Two later chunks confirm both readings without resolving them: chunk 029 asked whether the book
  treats Madho separately, seeing only the index parenthesis; chunk 030 answered that it does, from
  the separate "مادھو میاں شیخ" line (p. 327, folio 315).

**Saint — Shaikh Husain (Lal Husain), entry no. 17**
- **Why "Lal"**: "طریق ان کا ملامتیہ تھا۔ پوشاک سرخ رکھتے تھے۔ اس لیے لال حسین مشہور ہوئے" — his path
  was *Malamati*; he wore red dress, and so became famous as Lal Husain (p. 54, folio 42). The
  editor glosses Malamatiyya as the name of a silsila of free-spirited sufis (p. 54, folio 42,
  حاشیہ 3).
- Khalifa of Shaikh Bahlul Daryai; famous as a man of *hal*, *jazb*, longing, taste, *wajd* and
  *sama* (p. 54, folio 42). The pir is also given **indirectly** elsewhere: Shaikh Musa Khokhar's
  fayz came from Shaikh Bahlol Daryai and "شیخ لال حسین لاہوری بسبب پیر بھائی ہونے کے ان کا کمال ادب
  کرتے تھے", Lal Husain showed him the utmost respect because they were pir-brothers (p. 273,
  folio 261) — so Bahlol Daryai is made his pir by implication there rather than by assertion.
- **Family origin**: his grandfather, (کلجس رائے) [OCR?], was a Hindu who accepted Islam in the time
  of Firoz Shah Tughlaq; his son Usman was a religious man who earned lawful sustenance
  "جولاہوں کے کام سے", by the work of weavers; Shaikh Husain was born in his house (p. 54, folio 42).
- **Childhood initiation, with a rival account.** Author: at the age of seven, reading the Quran with
  a learned man named Abu Bakr, Shaikh Bahlul happened to pass through that mosque and his glance so
  fell on Husain that he became a perfect *wali* while still a child (p. 54, folio 42). Editor:
  *Haqiqat al-Fuqara* instead has him become the pupil of Hafiz Abu Bakr **at the age of ten, in
  955 AH** (p. 54, folio 42, حاشیہ 4).
- **His turn to the Malamati path**, from *Haqiqat al-Fuqara* 58–64: at first an orthodox Muslim, he
  was reading Tafsir-e Madarik with his teacher Shaikh Saad Allah Lahori (d. 999 AH / 1590 CE) when
  the teacher explained "وما الحیوٰۃ الدنیا الا لہو ولعب" and Shah Husain understood the world to be
  mere play; he told his teacher "مجھے حال درکار ہے قال نہیں" — I need *hal*, not *qal* — abandoned
  the student's life and took to the Malamati way. The editor adds that Saad Allah himself often
  voiced the Malamati school, quoting Nizam al-Din Ahmad on him: "بروش ملامتیہ سلوک می نمود"
  (Tabaqat-e Akbari, Lucknow, 391) (p. 56, folio 44, حاشیہ 1).
- **Repentance at the end of life**: it is a fact that in his last years he repented of all acts
  contrary to the shariah, became regular in fasting and prayer, and died in that state (Abdullah
  Khweshgi Qasuri, *Maarij al-Wilayat*, ms folio 519b) (p. 56, folio 44, حاشیہ 1).
- **Death year — three readings, and the editor rejects the book's.** Author: death **1008 AH**, in
  Shah Akbar's reign (pp. 55–56, folios 43–44), followed by a chronogram quatrain ending
  "سال ترحیل شمع عشق حسین" with **"۹۴۵ھ [OCR?]" printed beside it** (p. 56, folio 44) — the same
  numeral the text gives for his **birth**. Editor, reaching a verdict by argument: all the local
  histories give 1008 AH / 1599 CE, of which *Haqiqat al-Fuqara* (1041 AH) is the oldest;
  *Miftah al-Arifin*, composed seven years later in 1048 AH, gives **1013 AH**, which makes the
  *Haqiqat al-Fuqara* figure doubtful; but the contemporary historian Badayuni recorded that while
  composing *Najat al-Rashid* in 999 AH / 1590 CE "I learnt at Lahore that Shah Husain, hearing the
  song of a sweet-voiced woman singer, became beside himself and fell from the roof of a house and
  died" (Najat al-Rashid p. 320). Preferring the contemporary to the later tazkira-writers:
  "تحقیق کا حاصل یہ ہے کہ شاہ حسین کا صحیح سال وفات ۹۹۹ھ ہے نہ کہ ۱۰۰۸ھ" — the correct death year is
  **999 AH, not 1008 AH** (p. 55, folio 43, حاشیہ 2).
- **Scale of his following, quantified**, quoted from *Haqiqat al-Fuqara*: nine thousand perfected
  servants and "ایک لاکھ پچپن ہزار" — one hundred and fifty-five thousand murids — of whom sixteen
  became famed khalifas, four titled Gharib, four Diwan, four Khaki and four Bilawal, with the
  burial place of each given: the Gharibs at Ratti Mittha next to Wazirabad, at لنگرہی [OCR?] in
  Wazirabad district, at جیلا پور [OCR?] in the Deccan, and beside Shah Husain's own grave; the
  Diwans — the first being Shaikh Madho — with Diwan Gorakh and Allah Diwan buried at Lahore and
  Diwan Bakhshi at Bijapur in the Deccan; the Khakis (Maula Bakhsh Khaki and Khaki Shah at Lahore,
  Khaki Shah at Wazirabad, Haidar Khaki in the Deccan); and the Bilawals (Shah Rang Bilawal, Budhu
  Bilawal and Shah Mast Bilawal at Lahore, Shah Bilawal in the Deccan) (p. 55, folio 43).
- **A named khalifa and a second-generation murid.** Entry ۲۳۰: Haji Jamiat's "بیعت بخدمت شاہ رنگ
  [OCR?] بلاول حضرت لال حسین لاہوری کے خلیفہ کی خدمت میں تھی" — his allegiance was to Shah
  Rang[OCR?] Bilawal, khalifa of Hazrat Lal Husain Lahori (p. 271, folio 259): a transmission line
  out of Shah Husain that the row can cite.
- **His writings**: two works have so far come to light — *Kafian* in Punjabi and *Risala-e Tahniyat*
  in Persian prose, presenting the problems of tasawwuf in an easy manner; the full text, edited and
  introduced by Muhammad Iqbal Mujaddidi, was printed in Majalla Maarif, Azamgarh, August 1970 and
  again in Sahifa, Lahore, July 1972 (p. 56, folio 44). Nine further sources are listed (pp. 56–57,
  folios 44–45), including Dara Shikoh's *Hasanat al-Arifin* pp. 18, 74, and the editor's own
  article "مادھو لال حسین" in the Urdu Daira-e Maarif-e Islamia of Punjab University. The
  bibliography repeats both: item ۲۲۲ "محمد اقبال مجددی : مادھو لال حسین مقالہ مشمولہ اردو دائرہ
  المعارف اسلامیہ دانش گاہ پنجاب لاہور" (p. 303, folio 291) and item ۱۰۳ "شاہ حسین لاہوری :
  تحقیقت[OCR?]۔ مرتبہ محمد اقبال مجددی مشمولہ مجلہ صحیفہ لاہور جولائی ۱۹۷۲ء" (p. 297, folio 285,
  title flagged); the editor's article list adds "۴- شاہ حسین لاہوری کی ایک غیر مطبوعہ فارسی تصنیف
  تہنیت۔ معارف اگست ۱۹۷۰ء" — an **unpublished Persian work titled "تہنیت"** known to him in
  manuscript, *Ma'arif*, August 1970 (p. 307, folio 295).

**Saint — Shaikh Madho, entry no. 24**
- Of Shaikh Husain Lahori's khalifas, "اپنے پیر روشن ضمیر کے محبوب و مطلوب تھے" — he was his pir's
  beloved and desired one (p. 64, folio 52).
- **Origin**: "قصبہ شاہدرہ میں ان کے باپ ماں بہ قوم کے برہمن تھے رہتے تھے" — his father and mother,
  Brahmins by caste, lived in **qasba Shahdara** (p. 64, folio 52).
- **Conversion**: "حضرت حسین ان کے حسن و جمال پر بدل و جان عاشق ہو گئے اور بجذب محبت اپنی طرف کھینچا"
  — Hazrat Husain fell in love with his beauty with heart and soul and drew him by the attraction of
  love; so Madho too came to him with sincere devotion "اور مشرف بہ اسلام ہوئے", and was honoured
  with Islam, and through training became a perfect wali (pp. 64–65, folios 52–53). Separately
  sourced by the editor: "میاں مادھو نے اسلام قبول کر لیا تھا" (Haqiqat al-Fuqara p. 82) (p. 65,
  folio 53, حاشیہ 1).
- **He became the shrine's mujawir**: "جب حسین رح نے وفات پائی۔ ان کے مزار پر مجاور رہے" — when
  Husain died he remained a *mujawir* at his mazar (p. 65, folio 53).
- **The joint shrine's one textual warrant**: the editor's footnote, sharpening the death to 22 Zi
  al-Hijja 1056 AH / 1646 CE, adds "اور اپنے شیخ کے احاطہ میں دفن ہوا" — **and he was buried within
  his shaikh's enclosure** (p. 65, folio 53, حاشیہ 2). That single clause is the book's warrant for
  the archive's joint shrine, and it is the **editor's**, not the author's.
- **The editor doubts Madho's very historicity** (chunk 005, entry no. 24, pp. 64–65). The argument
  itself sits under that chunk's *Arguments and interpretations* heading and is **not carried in this
  CSV** — read it on the page before the row is rewritten.

**Shrine / Darbar**
- Shah Husain's "مزار گوہر بار لاہور میں ہے", his mazar is in Lahore (pp. 55–56, folios 43–44). No
  quarter is named in the entry; the street address comes only from the appendix
  (مادھو لال حسین سٹریٹ باغبانپورہ, p. 287, folio 275).
- Chunk 026 records **nothing on the shrine, the Mela Chiraghan, Madho, or the death year**, and the
  Malamati affiliation is not stated there (pp. 271, 273).
- The place index's "شالامار باغ | ۱۲۲" (p. 334, folio 322) → PDF p. 134 names **the garden only**,
  and its co-indexed names — "محمد سعید نقشبندی لاہوری | ۱۲۲", "محمد یعقوب مجددی | ۱۲۲",
  "محمد ہاشم کشمی | … ۱۲۲ …", "محمد تقی سرہندی | ۱۲۲" (pp. 328–329) — make it a Mujaddidi page,
  **not obviously the saint's**. Do not read a Mela Chiraghan location out of it.

**Legends & Miracles**
- **Shrine practice recorded in a footnote**: Shaikh Muhammad Tahir Lahori (d. 1040 AH), an extremely
  shariah-bound khalifa of the Mujaddid Alf-e Sani, is quoted as saying "اگر مجھے علمائے ظاہر کے
  طعنوں کا خدشہ نہ ہوتا تو میں اکثر شیخ حسین کے مزار پر جا کر استمداد کرتا" — "If I did not fear the
  taunts of the exoteric ulama, I would often go to Shaikh Husain's mazar and seek help"
  (Akhbar al-Auliya, ms folio 57ab). The editor takes this both as confirmation of the repentance
  and as "بین ثبوت", clear proof, that Shah Husain was an orthodox Muslim whose *wilayat* was
  accepted (p. 56, folio 44, حاشیہ 1).

**Significance**
- **The editor of this edition is himself a published authority on the saint** (pp. 303, 307), and
  the colophon gives his post as "لیکچرر تاریخ شاہ حسین کالج لاہور" — lecturer in history at **Shah
  Husain College, Lahore** (p. 306, folio 294): a Lahore institution bearing the saint's name, which
  the chunk does not connect to the shrine.
- Folio 52, shared between the two saints, also carries "عبد الحکیم سیالکوٹی | ۵۲ ، ۱۹۶",
  "محمد بخش میاں | ۳۵ ، ۵۲", "ظہیر الدین احمد | ۴۶ ، ۵۲ ، ۹۲" (pp. 320–327) — leads only.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

**B14. `madho-lal-hussain` — three death years, a fourth numeral in the wrong place, and the joint
burial never stated.** Shah Husain: text **1008 AH**; *Miftah al-Arifin* **1013 AH**; editor concludes
**999 AH** (p. 55, folio 43 and حاشیہ 2). The chronogram quatrain closing the entry is printed with
"**۹۴۵ھ [OCR?]**" beside it (p. 56, folio 44) — the year the text gives for his **birth**; it must not
be read as a death date. Age at first instruction: seven in the text, ten in 955 AH per
*Haqiqat al-Fuqara* (p. 54, folio 42 and حاشیہ 4) (chunk 004). Madho: birth 983, death 1056, age 73 —
internally consistent, but against the editor's 999 for Shah Husain it leaves Madho as mujawir for 57
years, and everything about Madho rests on *Haqiqat al-Fuqara*, **the book the editor elsewhere calls
careless and uncritical** (chunk 005). **The book names شیخ مادھو as the first of Shah Husain's four
Diwan khalifas and his "معشوق و محبوب" and the editor's article is titled "مادھو لال حسین"
(pp. 55, 57; folios 43, 45), but no chunk states that the two are buried together or that a single
shrine holds them.** Confirm at Madho's own entry, printed p. 52.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "ملک پنجاب", "پنجاب"; refers to: the author's own time, "اس وقت", and the book's scope; author's text (1875), the preface "در اظہار باعث تالیف کتاب حدیقۃ الاولیاء"): the author says friends asked him to write "ایک اور مجموعہ مختصر اردو زبان میں ... جس میں ملک پنجاب کے اولیاء کا حال ہو۔ یعنی دہلی سے پشاور تک جس قدر علاقہ اس وقت پنجاب کے ساتھ متعلق ہے اور مشہور اولیاء کے مزار اس میں ہیں". The book defines its own unit: "پنجاب" as everything "from Delhi to Peshawar" that belonged to Punjab at the time of writing. This is a differently bounded unit in the book's own words, and both ends it names lie outside today's province (p. 37, folio 25).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کی سرزمین"; refers to: the author's selection rule for the Naqshbandi chaman, period not stated; author's text (1875)): after giving the Naqshbandi chain down to "خواجہ باقی باللہ دہلوی", the author says: "چونکہ بزرگان مرقوم الصدر سے کسی کا مزار پنجاب کی سرزمین میں نہ تھا۔ لہذا احوال مفصل ان کا قلم انداز ہوا اور خواجہ باقی باللہ سے آغاز کیا گیا". In other words, a tomb in Punjab is his test for inclusion (p. 127, folio 115).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the arrival of the Qadiri line, which the book places after his ancestors left Baghdad at the time of "ہلاکو خاں". No year is given in the text; author's text (1875)): "حضرت کے سلسلہ میں سے حضرت سید محمد غوث اوچی جیلی گیلانی اول پنجاب میں آئے". The same passage says he came "خراسان کے راستے ملتان میں" and settled "اوچ کے مقام پر". The editor's footnote (modern) on the arrival gives "بقول علی اصغر گیلانی آپ ۸۸۷ھ میں اوچ میں داخل ہوئے" (citing "خزینۃ الاصفیا ۱ / ۱۱۶ بحوالہ شجرۃ الانوار"). That date is the editor's, not the author's (p. 40, folio 28).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the lifetime of "شاہ معروف چشتی و قادری", who is entry 12; no date is stated in the hit; author's text (1875)): the Naushahi order spread in Punjab through him: "انہیں کی ذات بابرکات سے پنجاب میں سلسلہ نوشاہیہ نے فروغ پایا". The pronoun may refer to Shah Ma'ruf or to "سید مبارک حقانی", named just before. The text does not settle which (p. 50, folio 38).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the author's present for the "thousands" and the lifetime of Naushah for the founding; author's text (1875), entry 30 "شیخ حاجی محمد قادری المشہور بحضرت نوشہ گنج بخش"): "طریقہ نوشاہیہ جس کے فقیر پنجاب میں ہزاروں ہیں۔ ان سے شروع ہوا" (p. 70, folio 58).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the time of "خواجہ معین الدین حسن سجزی ثم الاجمیری", period not stated; author's text (1875), opening of the Chishti chaman): "یہ سلسلہ ہند اور پنجاب میں حضرت خواجہ معین الدین حسن سجزی سے شائع ہوا" (p. 85, folio 73).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the lifetime of Khwaja Sulaiman Taunsvi, "ولادت ۱۱۸۴ھ ... وفات ۱۲۶۷ھ" as the footnote gives them; editor's footnote (modern), on the khalifas of Nur Muhammad Maharvi): "پنجاب میں سلسلہ چشتیہ نظامیہ فخریہ کی زیادہ اشاعت حضرت خواجہ سلیمان تونسوی کے ذریعہ ہوئی" (p. 120, folio 108).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the time of "احمد شاہ ابدالی", as the footnote states; editor's footnote (modern), on Ghulam Murtaza Qasuri, citing "رسالہ مسائل فقہ قلمی مملوکہ حجی [OCR?] معین الدین لاہور"): "آپ نے پنجاب میں سکھ گردی سے تنگ آکر پشاور ہجرت فرمائی"; "احمد شاہ ابدالی جب پنجاب میں آیا تو اس نے یہاں کے علماء سے ایک مسئلہ کے بارے دریافت کیا". Ghulam Murtaza's name is among the ulama who answered (p. 152, folio 140).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "خطہ"; refers to: the historiography of the region up to the editor's day, with no date given; editor's introduction (modern)): "اس اعتبار سے پنجاب سب سے محروم القسمت خطہ ہے۔ یہاں کی اب تک کوئی باقاعدہ اور تحقیقی تاریخ نہیں لکھی گئی"; those who wrote on "پنجاب کی تاریخ" gave only formal chapters and did not touch the Sufi tazkiras; the need is to fill the gaps in "پنجاب کی سیاسی، ثقافتی اور رُوحانی تاریخ" (p. 23, folio 11).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the editor's day, with no date given; editor's introduction (modern)): the editor has compiled a list of tazkiras, malfuzat and maktubat of "پنجاب کے صوفیہ کرام", titled "پنجاب کی تاریخ تصوف کے مآخذ". No comprehensive tazkira of "پنجاب کے صوفیہ" has come to his notice. The Mufti's general tazkiras gave Punjab representation, and without them "آج ہمیں پنجاب میں کوئی عالم و عارف نظر ہی نہ آتا" (p. 24, folio 12).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سلطان محمود غزنوی سے لے کر ۱۲۹۲ھ / ۱۸۷۵ء تک", the span as the editor states it; editor's introduction (modern)): the book holds "پنجاب کے ۲۴۴ [OCR?] صوفیہ کرام کے حالات" in seven chamans (p. 31, folio 19). It also takes in some well-known Sufis "جن کا پنجاب سے کوئی تعلق نہیں تھا" (p. 32, folio 20).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "اکابر پنجاب"; refers to: the Sikh period as the author witnessed it, with no dates given; editor's introduction (modern), assessing the book): among the book's merits, the editor says the Mufti recorded "پنجاب کے اعیان کی سکھوں کے ہاتھوں بے عزتی اور مشائخ کے مقابر کی تباہی" as an eyewitness, and that his own family had been a target of "سکھ گردی". By preserving his contemporaries he did "پنجاب پر بڑا احسان" (p. 33, folio 21). Some Punjab shaikhs are found only in this book (p. 32, folio 20). Among the book's weaknesses, the editor says the Mufti wrote up whatever "اکابر پنجاب" came to mind "ایک نشست میں" (p. 33, folio 21).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the book's scope; title page and table of contents of the modern edition): the subtitle reads "پنجاب کے اکابر صُوفیہ کا مُستند تذکرہ" (p. 1 and p. 9, folio not stated). The table of contents heads a closing section "حالات بزرگان پنجاب / جن کی وفات کا سن و تاریخ بپایہ ثبوت نہیں پہنچا" (entries ۲۰۵ onward) (p. 20, folio 8).


- (book's word: "پنجاب کا صوبہ", "پنجاب"; refers to: the Ghaznavid period — "سلطان محمود کے مرنے کے بعد جب مسعود تخت نشین ہوا", then Maudud — as the book states; author's text (1875)): Masud's young son Majdud "پنجاب کا صوبہ قرار پایا", with Ayaz appointed as his tutor (اتالیق). Here `صوبہ` names the office Majdud was given, not a bounded unit. After Masud died and Maudud took the throne, Majdud rebelled and set up "اپنی سلطنت علیحدہ ہندوستان میں". Maudud marched on him, Majdud died suddenly on the day of Eid during the siege of Lahore, "اور مودود نے اپنا تسلط پنجاب میں کر لیا" (p. 193, folio 181).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سلطان مسعود غزنوی کے وقت", death given as "سال چار سو اڑتالیس ۴۴۸"; author's text (1875), in a passage that opens "صاحب کتاب تحفۃ الواصلین لکھتا ہے کہ" — where the citation ends is not marked): Shaikh Muhammad Ismail Muhaddith Lahori, a Bukhari sayyid, settled in Lahore and taught hadith and tafsir. He was the first of the callers to Islam to come to Lahore, thousands converted, "اور دین اسلام ان کے طفیل سے پنجاب میں شائع ہوا" (p. 191, folio 179).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: modern historiography, period not stated; editor's footnote (modern), on the entry above): "صاحب نزہۃ الخواطر جن کا پنجاب کے سلسلہ میں سب سے بڑا ماخذ خزینۃ الاصفیا ہے اور انہوں نے عموماً خزینۃ الاصفیا کی روایات بغیر کسی تحقیق کے شامل کتاب بھی کر لی ہیں". In the same footnote the editor calls the author's source "تحفۃ الواصلین ایک وضعی تذکرہ" and concludes "شیخ اسمٰعیل محدث لاہوری کا صحیح سال وفات ۹۸۰ ھ ہے نہ کہ ۴۴۸ ھ" (pp. 191–192, folios 179–180).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the Ghaznavid sultanate in Punjab up to "شاہ بہرام کے وقت"; quoted source: تحفۃ الواصلین, as retold in the author's text (1875) under the Shahid Ganj entry): "جب سلطنت سلاطین غزنویہ کی پنجاب میں ہوگئی اور لاہور دار الحکومت قرار پایا تو مدت تک سلاطین غزنویہ کی سلطنت بخوبی پنجاب میں رہی۔ آخر جب شاہ بہرام کے وقت آپس میں سلاطین غزنویہ کے فساد ہوا تو پنجاب کی حکومت بالکل ضعیف ہوگئی". Raja Anangpal then took Lahore, and "چند سے [OCR?]" there was Hindu rule until an army from Ghazni returned (p. 264, folio 252). The editor's separate verdict on this source is in the previous bullet (p. 192, folio 180).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "ملک پنجاب"; refers to: Chingiz Khan's pursuit of "شہزادہ جلال الدین خوارزمی", date not stated; quoted source: "کتاب تذکرہ حمیدیہ", as reported in the author's text (1875) under the Bibiyan Pakdaman entry): "چنگیز خان کی فوج اس کے تعاقب میں پنجاب میں داخل ہوئی۔ تمام ملک پنجاب انہوں نے غارت کر لیا۔ شہر لاہور کے لوگ دو مہینے تک ان کے ساتھ لڑتے رہے". The author adds that this account "مشہور نہیں ہے۔ شاید کوئی اس پر یقین نہ کرے گا۔ مگر عجب نہیں ہے کہ ایسا ہوا ہو" (p. 248, folio 236). The editor's addenda give a separate reading for Lahore only: "شہاب الدین نسوی ... نے چنگیز خان کے لاہور پر حملے کا ذکر ہی نہیں کیا" (p. 352, folio 340).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the Sikh disorders, "قوم سکھ", date not stated; Karam Shah's death is given both as "سال ایک ہزار دو سو" in words and as "۱۲۰۱ ھ" in the chronogram line; author's text (1875)): Shaikh Karam Shah Qureshi had lived in Lahore. "جب غارت گر ملا [OCR?] قوم سکھ نے پنجاب میں ہنگامہ غارت گری گرم کیا تو یہ بزرگ لکھنؤ کو چلا گیا". He was killed by bandits near Shahjahanpur on the way back (p. 190, folio 178).
- (book's word: "پنجاب" in "بعد غارت پنجاب"; refers to: "جب احمد بادشاہ درانی پہلی مرتبہ لاہور کی تسخیر کو آیا"; author's text (1875), introduced "نقل ہے"): "جب بعد غارت پنجاب بادشاہ کابل کو چلا گیا". A Lahore man then told the saint (Haji Muhammad Said Lahori) that "افغانوں" had looted his house and carried off his daughter to Kabul (p. 214, folio 202). The editor's footnote on the same page dates the first attack on Lahore only: "احمد شاہ ابدالی کا لاہور پر پہلا حملہ (۱۱۶۱ھ) ۱۷۴۸ء کو ہوا".
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "۱۱۶۱ھ/۱۷۴۸ء" to "۱۱۸۷ھ/۱۷۷۳ء", as the footnote states; editor's footnote (modern)): rejecting the date the author gives for Ahmad Shah Durrani's farman to Mufti Muhammad Mukarram ("۱۱۲۰ھ [OCR?]"), the editor writes "یہ سنہ غلط ہے۔ احمد شاہ ابدالی نے ۱۱۶۱ھ/۱۷۴۸ء میں پنجاب پر مختلف حملے شروع کیے اور ۱۱۸۷ھ/۱۷۷۳ء میں فوت ہو گیا" (p. 277, folio 265).
- (book's word: "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ والی پنجاب"; refers to: Ranjit Singh's rule, date not stated; Mastan Shah's death given as "سال ایک ہزار دو سو ستتر ہجری"; author's text (1875)): "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ والی پنجاب کو کمال اعتقاد اس کی نسبت تھا". He offered thousands of rupees, which the majzub Mastan Shah refused (p. 246, folio 234).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کے مالک فرنگی"; refers to: the day "جس روز راجہ رنجیت سنگھ مرا تھا", with "نو برس" more of "یہ سلطنت" — the book's "سکھوں کی سلطنت"; author's text (1875), reporting the majzub Taj Shah's saying): "سکھوں کی سلطنت کی خرابی کا حال پہلے ہی اس نے بیان کر دیا تھا ... نو برس اور یہ سلطنت رہے گی پھر پنجاب کے مالک فرنگی ہو جائیں گے". The unit here is the book's "سکھوں کی سلطنت", passing to "فرنگی" (p. 244, folio 232).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "ملک پنجاب"; refers to: the book's own scope, written in the author's present; the closing chronograms read "۱۲۹۲ھ"; author's text (1875)): chaman 5 will hold "ان بزرگوں کا ذکر ... جو پنجاب میں عالم و فاضل صاحب نام گذرے ہیں" (p. 191, folio 179). Chaman 7 is headed "عَورات صالحات کے ذکر میں جو پنجاب میں گذر چکی ہیں" (p. 247, folio 235). A later section is headed "حالات بزرگان پنجاب جن کی وفات کا سن و تاریخ بپایہ ثبوت نہیں پہنچا" (p. 261, folio 249). In the closing, the author says his Persian *خزینۃ الاصفیا* was too large, so "شائقین ملک پنجاب کا یہ مطلب حاصل نہیں ہوتا تھا کہ کتنے بزرگ پنجاب کے ملک میں صاحب طریقت گذرے ہیں", a difficulty this short Urdu book removes (p. 280, folio 268).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the book's scope, chronogram "۱۲۹۲ھ"; quoted source: chronogram "از مفتی غلام صفدر صاحب لاہوری خلف مؤلف"): "جو خدا کے دوست تھے پنجاب میں   حال ان کا اس سے ہوتا ہے عیان" (p. 282, folio 270).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the scholars of Punjab "شیخ حامد سے لے کر حضرت شیخ بہاؤالدین زکریا ملتانی تک"; editor's footnote (modern)): Maulana Muhammad Aqil Lahori's *تحفۃ المسلمین* records Shaikh Hamid Qari's sayings and also gives "پنجاب کے اکابر علما کے حالات" across that span. The editor says its rare manuscript is in his own library (p. 278, folio 266).

## From Maulvi Nur Ahmad Chishti, Tahqiqat-e Chishti

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/tahqiqat_chishti.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint**
  - Section title "ذکر احوال مزار پر انوار و خانقاہ عالیجاہ حضرت ... مادھو لال حسین لاہوری", the first entry under the Lahore heading (p. 45); later title "حضرت مادھو لعل حسین", and "حضرت شیخ مادھو[OCR?] و جناب حسین", "عاشق و معشوق" (p. 80).
  - Source: "کتاب حقیقت الفقرا" by "شیخ پیر محمد صاحب", "نام تاریخی" "شیخ محمود[OCR?]" (p. 45); credited again (pp. 63, 69, 70, 72 — "بقول شیخ پیر محمد رضا[OCR?] کتاب حقیقتہ الفقرا").
  - Name: "حضرت کا نام مشہورہ ڈھاڈا[OCR?] حسین ہے[OCR?]", "ڈھاڈا[OCR?]" "راجپوتوں میں ایک ذات"; paternal descent "کلشتر[OCR?]", an ancestor who became Muslim titled "شیخ الاسلام"; father "حضرت کے والد کا نام مشہور عثمان[OCR?] تھا[OCR?]", "پیشہ بافندگی" (p. 45).
  - Birth: only a chronogram from Pir Muhammad, digits "۵۰[OCR?]" in an illegible line, "در شمار عدد و چہل و [illegible] و پنجہ[OCR?]" (p. 45); the author's own verse "بسال مولودش از سروش آل[OCR?] ..." (p. 46). Year (not stated legibly). A garbled line "مسیح[OCR?] صاحب[OCR?] برواح[OCR?] نفرد[OCR?] مثنویہ[OCR?] ۹۳۵[OCR?]" — what "۹۳۵[OCR?]" refers to is unclear (p. 46).
  - Bai'at chain: Lal Husain → "حضرت بہلول" → "حضرت شہ[OCR?] لطیف بری[OCR?]" → "شاہ محمد مقیم[OCR?]" → "حضرت حیات المیر[OCR?] قادری[OCR?]", "نبیرہ حضرت غوث الاعظم"; lives in "مجموعہ[OCR?] ہشتم[OCR?]" (pp. 46–47).
  - Boyhood: "حضرت لال حسین دہ سالہ تھی[OCR?]" when Bahlul came; memorising with Maulvi Abu Bakr, "ساکن [illegible]", seventh sipara done (p. 50); his maktab in "وہ محلہ [illegible] دروازہ ٹکسالی[OCR?] لاہور متصل[OCR?] [illegible] دریا" (p. 50). Outside "دروازہ ٹکسالی [OCR?]", where Takia Sher Ali Shah now is, in the time of "بابر بادشاہ و اکبر بادشاہ" a mohalla held "حضرت حسین کی والدہ کا دولتخانہ"; in its mosque "حضرت حسین مرحوم مولوی [illegible] ابو بکر [OCR?] کے پاس سبق قرآن شریف [illegible] کا پڑھا کرتے تھے" (p. 101).
  - Bahlul's coming: date by "حضرت پیر محمد صاحب حقیقت الفقر[OCR?]", "سال پنجاہ و پنج و نہ صد بود", no era; a second chronogram "لمحررہ[OCR?]" illegible (p. 52). Tarawih: on the seventh day he had recited all he knew (p. 50); completed the Quran in Ramadan (pp. 51–52).
  - Routine: "دائم الصوم اور قایم اللیل"; dawn and ishraq at Hujwiri's khanqah and mazar[OCR?]; "بارہ برس" there (p. 53). Later: Quran to chasht, "قیلولہ"[OCR?], teaching tafsir[OCR?] to asr, at night in the river (p. 54). Tafsir with "شیخ سعد اللہ", "تفسیر مدارک[OCR?] و نسفی[OCR?]"; the "لہو و لعب" verse, dancing, dispute (pp. 54–55); left the madrasa dancing (p. 55).
  - "اس روز سے آپ نے طریقہ ملامتیہ اختیار کر لیا تا لوگ اوسے نفرت کریں" (p. 56); chronogram year illegible, age "[illegible: numeral word][OCR?] برس" (p. 56). Wine and music; Bahlul satisfied "حضرت حسین باحق واصل ہیں" (p. 56). After Bahlul's death "بدستور لاہور میں مرجع خلق عام رہے[OCR?]"; danced to chang and rabab "واسطے اخفائے[OCR?] حال"; the Quran every night in the first watch (p. 57).
  - Sayings: on فقر (ف = فقر و فاقہ و فنا, ق = قناعت and قرب حق, ر = ریاضت and رضا) (p. 46); the sajjada nashin holds a بیاض "بدست خط خاص اونکے" on the five letters of درویش (e.g. د = درد دل, ش = شکر حق and شرم) (p. 46).
  - At Akbar's court (citing "کتاب حقیقت الفقرا[OCR?]"): "داڑہی منڈا کر لباس لال سرح[OCR?] پہنتا ہی اور شراب پی کر رقص کنان[OCR?] [illegible] پہر تا ہی[OCR?]", "اور دعوے ولایت کرنا ہو" (p. 63). Devotees: "شاہزادہ سلیم"; "شاہ ابہال[OCR?] اور شاہ مراد"; "خواجہ دولت خاں", "خاں خاناں[OCR?]", "میر عدل[OCR?]", "ابو الفضل[OCR?]", "شیخ عبد الرحمن[OCR?]", "صادق خاں[OCR?]", "شہباز خان[OCR?]", "راجہ [illegible]"; "آپ کسی کی طرف کوئی التجا نہ لاتے تہے"; Akbar "دل جان سی آپ کا خادم ہوا" (p. 65). "حضرت نور الدیں جہانگیر بادشاہ" at the head of the miracles; someone appointed to write down his words; a book of his "حالات" (p. 59).
  - Companions of the majlis: "میاں[OCR?] شعبان", "میاں[OCR?] ابراہیم[OCR?]", "ملا محمد[OCR?]", "یعقوب[OCR?]", "شاہ[OCR?] جہاں[OCR?]", "محمد[OCR?] اسلام[OCR?]", "شہاب[OCR?] الدیں", "صالح[OCR?]" (pp. 58–59).
  - Death ("حال وفات حضرت حسین رحمتہ اللہ علیہ"): age "[illegible: a number in words] سال"; credited to "صاحبِ تحقیق[OCR?] الفقرا[OCR?]"; "اکبر[OCR?] [illegible] سنہ [illegible]"; carried across "دریائے راوی"; "ہمیں اب جانا الہٰی چاہیئے[OCR?] وصال میں طلب فرماتے ہیں" (p. 69). Buried where "کہ آپ نے خود پسند فرمائی"; date "روز جمعہ سلخ ماہ جمادی الثانی سنہ ایک ہزار آٹھ[OCR?]" from "کتاب حقیقتُ الفقرا[OCR?]"; chronogram "گفت ہاتف کہ مستِ عشقِ ازل", a qit'a with "چشتیا[OCR?]" (p. 70). "ماتمِ حسین میں شورِ کربلا پنجاب میں برپا ہو گیا تھا" (p. 70).
  - Contemporaries and way: "سیر العارفین میں مذکور ہے کہ حضرت حسو تیلی اور حضرت مادہو لال حسین ہم عہد"; "طریقہ حضرت لال حسین کا مخذ[OCR?] و بانہ[OCR?] قلندرانہ تھا" (p. 610). Named second of the khalifas of "حضرت بلاول دریائی [OCR?]" — "حضرت موسی کہوکر [OCR?]", "حضرت [illegible] مادھو لال حسین صاحب [OCR?]", "حضرت [illegible] حنبلی [OCR?]", "حضرت ارزانی [OCR?] دیوان [OCR?]" (p. 417); someone ("یہ مست") went to him and "وہ خاموش [OCR?] رہے [OCR?]", with "لاکھ [OCR?] روپیہ" and a "لشکر [OCR?]" in a damaged passage (p. 418). End of a chain "… اور وہ رنگ لا دل[OCR?] صاحب کے اور وہ حضرت مادھو لعل دیوان کے …", subject "بیبی[OCR?] شاہ" (p. 334).
- **Saint (Madho)**
  - "حضرت مادہو ایک حسین لڑکا ذات کا برہمن تہا", "ساکن قصبہ شاہدرہ[OCR?]"; Husain circled his house at night (p. 57). "سولہ[OCR?] برس", Husain "بدنام"; Madho turned to him (p. 58); "آخر کار مادہو ظاہر بہی مسلمان ہو گیا" (p. 58); "بصدق[OCR?] دل مسلمان ہو گیا" on the day of the Ganga miracle, age "[illegible: numeral] سال[OCR?]" (p. 59); Husain gave him wine, "کدورت کفر اوسکے[OCR?] دل سے رفع کی" (p. 60); at "بابو یورہ[OCR?]" made "ولی کامل" through drink (p. 60). Also "جب وہ اٹھارہ برس کے ہوئے تو مشرف با سلام ہوئے", then "فنا فی اللہ" (p. 72).
  - Basant: on Basant Panchami[OCR?] near Holi, Madho threw colour and gulal; Husain kept Basant every year for life (p. 58).
  - Service with "راجہ مان سنگہ[OCR?]", to Delhi and, "بحکم اکبر شاہ", the Deccan[OCR?] campaign; "آج سی[OCR?] میں آپ کا مرید ہوں" (p. 60). After Husain's death he wept "شب و روز حضرت کی قبرِ مبارک کو بغل میں لیکر" (p. 70); sent in a dream to "راجہ مان سنگھ" for twelve years, "بمضمونِ المامور معذور"; the Raja: "میں آپ کا نوکر بلکہ غلام ہوں", "میں آپ کا مرید و مخلص خاص ہوں" (p. 70); the author's "تنبیہ" on why (p. 70); "راجہ مر گیا" after twelve years (p. 71).
  - After "تیرہ برس کامل" Madho "مزارِ مطہر پر سجادہ نشیں ہو بیٹھے[OCR?]", became "ہم شکل حضرت حسین کے" (p. 71). Died "عمر اُن کی بہتر[OCR?] سال"; birth chronogram "سالِ میلاد اور[OCR?] ز رومی عدد ۲۲[OCR?] / سہ[OCR?] ہشتاد[OCR?] و بود و بر نہ صد[OCR?]" (year not computed); death "سنِ ایک ہزار [illegible: number word] ہجری ماہ ذی الحجج کی بائیسویں[OCR?] و روزِ دو شنبہ"; grave "پہلوئے مزارِ حضرت حسین" (p. 72).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - Location: "شمال رویہ[OCR?] موضع باغبان پورہ و غرب[OCR?] [illegible] باغ شالامار" (p. 45); near "شالا [OCR?] باغ [OCR?]" and in "باغباں پورہ [OCR?]" (chunk 008). "یہہ مقام بابو پورہ[OCR?] حضرت کو بہت[OCR?] پسند تہا" (p. 61); reburial "یہاں جہاں اب مزارِ مقدس واقعہ ہی" (p. 71).
  - Precinct (pp. 97–99): south of the large western building a deorhi, dalans, a white chuna-plastered hujra, a marble slab "این مکان ز بندہ غلام رسول[OCR?] حسین[OCR?] [illegible]"; "یہ جگہ مقام گدی ہے سجادہ نشین یہاں بیٹھنا ہے"; "مقام [illegible] حضرت مادہو" five by three gaz; "کہ یہاں حضرت مادہو رہتی تھی[OCR?]" (verb as printed); a women's quarter with three dalans; a well with a working charkhi (p. 97). A walled area "ایکسو[OCR?] پانچ گز" by "ایکسو[OCR?] گز"; a platform with ten graves, at its west end "کرم حسین[OCR?] سجادہ نشیں"; sajjada nashins' graves in order "سائیں واحد شاہ", "عظمت شاہ", "عابد شاہ", "طالب شاہ", "سائیں لال دین[OCR?] شاہ", "حیدر[OCR?] شاہ"; south of "صوبہ شاہ" "رحمتہ[OCR?] سائیں موت[OCR?] شاہ"; "سائیں غلام علی شاہ", "خانم[OCR?] محمد شاہ"; "ساتہہ[OCR?] ستر[OCR?] قبریں", "ایک ہزار[OCR?] [illegible]"; an eight-sided enclosure built "[illegible] با وہ سو ستر[OCR?] میں", with sloping kanguras, four corner seats, five arched doors on three sides, a half-circle door on the north (p. 98). Enclosure "سترہ[OCR?] گز مربع"; the platform "جسکو مقام تخت کہتے میں", one and a half gaz high, "ساڑھے[OCR?] بارہ[OCR?] گز" square; "تعویذ مبارک حضرت حسین علیہ الرحمتہ", two feet, "اوسپر ہمیشہ غلاف [illegible] رہتا ہے"; to the east "مزار حضرت حسین مزار پر انوار حضرت مادہو کی ہے"; no ghilaf on Madho's grave at the time of writing, "بروز عرس و سیر بھی غلاف ڈالا جاتا ہے"; upper level behind a marble door "ہمیشہ یہ مقفل رہتا ہے"; an octagonal white chiragdan "بفاصلہ سو[OCR?] گز" north, "چار گز" high, "اوسمین ہر روز چراغ روشن" (p. 99).
  - Zakariya Khan's mosque nearby: opens "مزار رویہ [illegible] مزار حضرت [illegible] و ہو [illegible] حسین[OCR?] صاحب" (p. 440); southern-arch inscription "نزد و درگاہ صاحب عرفان", "آنکہ معروف شدہ بہ لال حسین" / "خاک نعلین او [illegible] سرمہ عین", "مسجد و چاہ را انکو تعمیر" (p. 441); "اب یہ مسجد بہ [illegible] حضرت مادھو لال حسین کے [illegible]"; "شہر[OCR?] در و سہ[OCR?] چاہ"; "چار دیواری [illegible] وحضرت مادھو لال حسین ایک درخت لسوڑا[OCR?] کھڑا ہے" (p. 442).
  - As a landmark: "حضرت سلیمان جسکی مزار متصل مادھو [OCR?] لال حسین کے ہی" (p. 348); a mohalla phrase "کا نام محلہ [illegible: 6 words] مادھو لال حسین [illegible: 3 words]" (p. 664); "خانقاہ حضرت ماہ[OCR?] مولانا حسین مرحوم کی" north of the Khwaja Ayaz mosque, "علیحدہ درج کتاب ہوا ہو چکا ہے" (p. 728). ToC: "۷۲۱[OCR?] | حال باغبان پورہ", "۷۲۷[OCR?] | حال آبادی[OCR?] [illegible] باغبان پورہ"; "۷۲۴[OCR?] | حال مسجد اندرون باغبان پورہ" is a named mosque (p. 6). The Baghbanpura material of chunk 066 is under Figures and Cross-cutting (pp. 723–730).
- **Practices & Events**
  - Sajjada nashins: "میاں سائیں محبوب شاہ" "بعہد مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ" (p. 101); seal "مہر کرم حسین [OCR?] سجادہ نشین مادھو لال حسین [OCR?]" on the testimony (p. 321); "ما دہو لال[OCR?] حسن[OCR?] مسمی کرم حسن[OCR?] و حسن دین[OCR?]" in the division of the sajjadagi income (p. 318); "در حضرت رنگ[OCR?] بلاول[OCR?] خادم و خلیفہ و سجادہ نشین حضرت مادھو لال حسین صاحب" (p. 333); "[illegible] شاہ سجادہ نشین مزار [illegible] مادہو لال حسین", "سرکردہ[OCR?] فقرا" of "[illegible] لاہور", then "تمام فقرا ناراض ہو گئے" (p. 663); "خادم صولی [OCR?] شاہ سجادہ نشین حضرت مادہو لعل حسین" (p. 776), in the deed "خادم قدوۃ الفقرا سائین صولی [OCR?] شاہ سجادہ نشین مزار مبارک ... جناب شاہ لعل حسین صاحب" (p. 780).
  - Jhuggi Shah served the mazar ("ہم مزار حضرت مادہو لعل حسین کو کر [OCR?]") and built a house there at his own cost, "مکان ثبوت [OCR?] شاہ والہ" (p. 777); as Sikh rule faltered he served "مسمات مای صاحب جان [OCR?] محبوبہ ومنکوحہ صوفی شاہ مرشد خود" there, returning to Dori Shah's site after her death and the British settlement (p. 777). The deed bars claims by "خادمان و یلار [OCR?] سجادہ نشینان شاہ علی حسین صاحب و یا کسی از سلسلہ فقیران حسین شاہی" (p. 781).
  - Sher Ali Shah "مادہو[OCR?] لال حسین سے درخواست[OCR?] کر کے حضرت[OCR?] [illegible] مکاں میں باندھا[OCR?] تھا", with a cross-reference (p. 662); "حال مفصل حال مادہو لال حسین میں مفصل [illegible: 2 words]" (p. 663). The Basant observance (p. 58) and the urs ghilaf (p. 99) as above.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - A majzub orders Bahlul "یہاں سے براہ لاہور کو جاؤ اور [OCR?] حسین کو راہ ہدایت[OCR?] دکھلاؤ" (p. 49); the river water and the prayer that he be "فقیر عارف" (p. 50).
  - Prophecy: after bathing he marked a place with "مصلا[OCR?] و تکیہ[OCR?]", ordered "سبزہ[OCR?] لگاؤ[OCR?]", said his body would be taken "بابو پورہ[OCR?] میں لے جاکر دفن کرینگے", that Madho would go away and return after "بارہ[OCR?] برس", and a faqir "بسجادہ[OCR?] نشین رہیگا"; a "میر اسحاق[OCR?]" named (p. 61).
  - Flood and empty grave: "دریائے راوی میں سیلاب آیا اور پانی حضرت حسین کی مزار تک [illegible] آیا"; "قبر درمیان سے خالی نکلی"; "محمد صالح نامی" found "ایک گلدستہ گلہائے[OCR?] ریحان کا"; a voice ordered burial "مقام بابو[OCR?] پورہ[OCR?] میں" (p. 71); chronogram by "پیر محمد" "سدِ گل[OCR?] گورِ گل[OCR?] رو حسین[OCR?]" (p. 71).
  - "حسب الدعا[OCR?] مے[OCR?] حضرت حسین لاہوری" the country of Thatta granted to the Khan-e Khanan (p. 68; see **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**). The Ganga miracle (p. 59). A broken passage pairing "حضرت لال حسین" with "حضرت حضور علی [OCR?] صاحب" — visits "اوسی راہ سے", a boy "جناب رسول خدا صلی اللہ علیہ وآلہ وسلم کی گود میں", Huzur Ali taking "اُس لڑکے کو گود میں", ending "ہو وہ حضرت لال حسین کا"; not reconstructable (p. 611).
  - "لا تعد و لا تحصی": the author could not finish writing them in a lifetime (p. 73).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- **Husain's death** (book's word: "پنجاب[OCR?]" first, then "پنجاب" unflagged; period: the time of Hazrat Husain's death, as the book places it; no separate period stated): at Husain's death there was hardly anyone in "پنجاب[OCR?] [illegible: 4 words]" who did not grieve; "ماتمِ حسین میں شورِ کربلا پنجاب میں برپا ہو گیا تھا" (p. 70).
- **Basant prizes** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the author's own time under "سرکارِ انگریزی"): "[illegible] گھوڑی پنجاب میں موجود ہیں"; context damaged (p. 80).
- **A prophecy** (book's word: "پنجاب[OCR?]"; period not stated): a sajjada nashin's prophecy that the boy "پنجاب[OCR?] میں فقر کا صوبہ دار ہوگا" (p. 84). The boy's place is "ضلع امرتسر[OCR?]", outside Pakistan under ruling (c)(3); Pass 1 recorded this only as the book's wording.
- **Ranjit Singh's time and the author's present** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the time of Maharaja Ranjit Singh, and the author's present under the "سرکار" for p. 95): "تمام پنجاب میں مشہور تھا" that the outward king is Maharaja Ranjit Singh, said of Subah Shah (p. 85); government attention to the khanqah would be "موجب خوشنودی خاطر رعایای پنجاب" (p. 95). Pass 1 calls both statements incidental to the shrine.
- **British conquest and administration** (book's word: "ملک پنجاب" / "ممالک پنجاب"; period: the British conquest of the Punjab and the administration that followed; no year given for the conquest): Rajab Ali Khan was Mir Munshi "بوقتِ فتح ملک پنجاب یعنی جب[OCR?] سرکار گردوں وقار انگلشیہ[OCR?] ... نے[OCR?] فتح کیا" (p. 137); his title "میر منشی ممالک پنجاب" (pp. 137, 141); he presented himself to the "لفٹ[OCR?] گورنر صاحب بہادر پنجاب" (p. 142).
- **The Rasul Shahi order** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the time of writing, "دم[OCR?] تحریر"; no year): the sect is "چنداں[OCR?] کثرت سے نہیں"; "تمام پنجاب میں بجز اس مکان کے کہ نہیں"; at the time of writing only "چار فقیر اس سلسلہ کے موجود ہیں", with no other house, takia or khanqah — though the book then names one more house near Lahore (p. 145).
- **Ali Mardan Khan** (book's word: "پنجاب", paired with "ہند"; period: "عہد شاہجہانی"; carried from "مرآۃ الہند"): he "بڑی سی عمارات عالیشان ہند و پنجاب میں بنوائیں"; his gardens remain his memorial in many places (p. 415).
- **A wedding custom** (book's word: "پنجاب", in "حسب دستور مسلمانان پنجاب"; period: the Naushah episode — no date given): the bride's family demanded "ایک سو روپیہ لاگون کا", in the story of the marriage of Hazrat Naushah's sahibzadi (p. 484).
- **The Sikh state** (book's word: "پنجاب"; "حکومت پنجاب"; period: the Sikh period, Ranjit Singh's reign and after his death): in "اٹھارہ سو پانچ[OCR?]", "جسونت راؤ ہولکر اور … امیر خان پنجاب کو آدمیں[OCR?]" — verb garbled, so whether they came to Punjab is not certain (p. 580); the Faqir family's mohalla is called "قطب یونان[OCR?]" by "لاہور میں تمام لوگ بلکہ پنجاب" (p. 585); on Ranjit Singh's death Kharak Singh sat on "کرسی حکومت پنجاب" (p. 586); Dhian Singh to Sher Singh: "انشاء اللہ تعالیٰ عنقریب آپ حاکم پنجاب ہو نگے" (p. 588).
- **1848–1849 and annexation** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the Sikh state under "مہاراجہ دلیپ سنگھ" up to annexation, years flagged): "سنہ ۱۸۴۸[OCR?]ء میں پھر ایک فساد و پنجاب میں واقع ہوا", traced through Multan, "ملک ہزارہ" and four hard battles; "۲۹[OCR?] مارچ سنہ ۱۸۴۹[OCR?]ء تمام ملک سلطنت انگریزی میں داخل ہو گیا" — the book says "تمام ملک", not "پنجاب"; the qita on Dalip Singh's departure: "چون شمع[OCR?] پنجاب از پنجاب رفت" and "نو گلی از گلشن پنجاب رفت" (p. 592).
- **The Jalali faqirs** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the author's own day, "اب کی زمانہ"; otherwise not stated): the claim that these faqirs are Chihil-tani is dismissed: "یہ داخل سخن ہندی مجہولان پنجاب ہے" (p. 623).
- **Female infanticide** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period not stated — present-tense custom): "دختر کشی[OCR?]" was an old custom among Baba Nanak's descendants through Lakhmi Chand (the "بید می[OCR?]"), who thought themselves "اشرف من المخلوقات" "پنجاب مین" and would give a daughter to no one, since "اس ملک کی رسم ہی کہ لڑکی کی والد لڑکے والوں کا تابعدار ہو جاتا ہی" (p. 685).
- **Shah Jahan's builder** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the reign of Shah Jahan): the "معمار" of "سنہلا باغ" was appointed "نیر[OCR?] عمارت پنجاب" (office title damaged), and on his death money was granted from the treasury for his tomb (p. 727).
- **British settlement** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the setting-up of British rule, "عملداری سرکار گردون وقار انگریزی"): British rule and a "بندوبست قانونی" were established "پنجاب مین"; after this Jhuggi Shah returned to Dori Shah's site and set about restoring it (p. 777).
- **The famine** (book's word: "ملک پنجاب"; period: Mai Bhagi's childhood, shortly after her marriage at age seven in "سنہ [illegible] ہجری"; not otherwise dated): a famine of "ملک پنجاب", "ڈہائی سیرا کال", in which "تمام ملک درہم برہم ہوگیا" and a mother would not look after her son; Mai Bhagi and her mother left "وٹالہ[OCR?]" because of it (p. 789).
- **Alexander** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: Alexander's invasion — ancient usage): Alexander's historians name Porus and "ابی سریر [OCR?]"; "انمین سے ایک ہستناپور اور دوسرا پنجاب مین رہا کرتا تہا", both Chandar Bansi; the book does not say which lived in Punjab (p. 799).
- **Balban and the Tughluqs** (book's word: "پنجاب", second instance "پنجاب [OCR?]"; period: the reign of the Delhi king whose vizier was Balban, and the end of the Khaljis / rise of Ghiyas-ud-Din Tughluq): Balban's nephew "شیر خان", posted to guard the Indus, "اوسنی پنجاب کو انکی حملون سے بچایا" (from the Mughals) and drove them out of Ghazni (p. 810); Ghazi Beg Tughluq, governor of Multan, gathered "فوج کثیر" "پنجاب [OCR?] مین" against Khusrau Malik (p. 814).
- **The Afghans under Alamgir II** (book's word: "پنجاب"; period: the reign of Alamgir II, the Afghan (Ahmad Shah Abdali) period; Pass 1: the 18th-century unit, boundaries not stated): "افغانون نے پنجاب پر حملہ کرکے ملتان اور لاہور اپنی قبضہ مین کرلیا", while Sikh strength began to grow; Ghazi al-Din "پہر پنجاب کی فتح کرنے کا ارادہ کیا" (p. 851). The first statement names Multan and Lahore, which also carry it under their place-level sets.

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

*Place-level attachment (ruling (a)): the book's "لاہور" / "شہر لاہور" / "شہر" / "نواحِ لاہور" / "دارالسلطنتہ لاہور" / "ضلع لاہور", the city in general, attached in 38 chunks (001, 002, 003, 007, 009, 010, 011, 014, 017, 019, 022, 024, 026, 029, 033, 038, 039, 045, 047, 049, 051, 052, 053, 058, 059, 065, 066, 067, 068, 069, 072, 073, 074, 075, 076, 077, 078, 079), the id set printed identically and in the same order every time. Nothing here is about any one of these 35 sites. Chunk 001's bullet carries no material of its own.*

**The book's scope and the survey of Lahore**
- The commissioner "مسٹر ولیم کولڈسٹریم [OCR?]" ordered the author "کہ حالاتِ عمارات و مزارات و مقابر و مساجد نواحِ لاہور مفصل تحریر کروں" — to write a detailed account of the buildings, mazars, tombs and mosques of Lahore's environs. The author calls the task very hard "کیونکہ صدہا سال کے حالات بہم پہونچانے [OCR?] [illegible] مشکل", because the history of hundreds of years had to be gathered (p. 28). Elsewhere he says Lala Amin Chand's work on Lahore's buildings and people was cut short when "مسٹر[OCR?] ولیم[OCR?] گولڈ اسٹریم[OCR?]" left for Shahjahanabad (p. 734).
- The book "مشعرِ احوالِ مقابرِ بزرگانِ اہلِ اسلام وغیرہ عمارات و [illegible] و مراسمِ قدیمہ [illegible] لاہور" (reports on the tombs of Muslim elders, other buildings and the old customs of Lahore), "لیکن فی الاصل تواریخِ [OCR?] اولیا [illegible] ہے", but at root it is a history of saints (p. 29).
- Mufti Ghulam Sarwar had long sought "حالاتِ حضرات نواحیِ لاہور" and could not get them, "کیونکہ حضرات سجادہ نشیناں [illegible] اطلاعِ حال اپنا [illegible]" (p. 31).
- The survey proper opens under the heading "آغاز احوال قبور و مزارات و مقبرہ ہائے بزرگان لاہور اول[OCR?]" (p. 45).
- A forward reference: "مکانات اہل[OCR?] دہلی[OCR?] شہر لاہور میں تحریر یہ ہوگا انشاء اللہ تعالی" (the houses of the people[OCR?] of Delhi[OCR?] in the city of Lahore will be written up, God willing) (p. 292).
- The author cites Lala Amin Chand's *Safarnama* ("مطبوعہ کوہ نور"), its account of the city ("حال شہر لاہور کے بیان میں بہ صفحہ با دن[OCR?]"), and disputes it (p. 733).
- The afterword gives the book's scope as "چارون طرف لاہور کے جسقدر مزار ہین بزرگان سلف کی یادگار ہین اون سب کی حالات"; claims no book had yet been written or printed that covered "آثار [OCR?] قدیمہ لاہور" in full; and says it covers "بزرگان ہنود و اہل اسلام" and that its "صلح کلی" respects every religion (p. 872).

**Foundation legends** (two versions, kept side by side)
- In the Shah Kamal section, introduced "کہتے[OCR?] ہیں": Raja Ram Chand had three sons; "ایک لو[OCR?] نے [illegible] لاہور بنایا" (one, Lo[OCR?], built Lahore); a second, name lost in "[illegible: 4 words]", "قصور[OCR?] آباد کیا"; the third, "مسمی راو[OCR?]", founded Mauza Rawan. Closes "واللہ اعلم" (p. 661).
- In the dynastic history: Raja Ramchandar had two sons, Kushu and Lohu; "لوہو نی شہر لاہور آباد کیا" (p. 795).

**Pre-Mughal rulers and invasions**
- When Sabuktigin first invaded Hind, in "سن [illegible] سو ستہتر [OCR?] ہجری", "لاہور مین راجہ جی پال حاکم تہا" (p. 804). Sabuktigin, having won the Afghans over, "لاہور و ملتان کو اپنے قبضہ مین کر لیا" (p. 804).
- Jaipal, defeated, asked that a trusted man of Sabuktigin's come with him to Lahore to collect the tribute; he came with "نواب خیر اللہ خان [OCR?]". At his court "بطرف چپ چہتری اور بطرف راست برہمن کہتری"; the Chhatris urged him to keep his word, the Brahmins not to pay; Jaipal broke the agreement and imprisoned the envoy. Sabuktigin "لاہور پر چڑہ آیا"; the rajas of Delhi, Ajmer and Kalinjar[OCR?] helped Jaipal, but the Hindus were defeated (p. 804).
- In Sultan Mahmud's time "خاندان توار [OCR?] سی ایک راجہ اوسا [OCR?] لاہور و قنوج کا حاکم تہا" (p. 804).
- Mas'ud, facing the Turkmans (Tughril Beg), was "لاچار لاہور کے طرف روانہ ہوا"; his army looted the treasury at the Indus ("دریا سندہ") and put the blinded Muhammad back on the throne (p. 807).
- In Maudud's time the Hindu rajas besieged Lahore, "جو دار الخلافت اہل اسلام کا تہا", for "سات [OCR?] مہینہ" until the besieged drove them off (p. 807).
- "خسرو شاہ ولد بہرام [illegible] لاہور مین آکر بعالم مجبوری دار السلطنت بنایا"; he ruled "سات [OCR?] برس تک لاہور مین"; his son Khusrau Malik succeeded. The Ghurids took "یہہ ملک" from Khusrau "بہزار مکر و فریب", and "پہر یہان بہی سلطنت غوریان کی ہوئی" (pp. 808–809).
- From "کتاب خلاصۃ التواریخ": after "جہاں [illegible]", Lahore lay deserted ("ویران پڑا رہا") for a long time, until an amir of "سلطان سلوک[OCR?]" settled it again; "کامراں" is named in the damaged continuation (p. 430). Settlement then grew (p. 431).

**Mughal Lahore**
- When Humayun returns to Hindustan, "حاکم لاہور تاتار خان" comes out against him; Bairam Khan overcomes him off guard (p. 823). After Hemu takes Delhi, "اکبر نے لاہور مین کچہہ جمعیت بہم پہونچا کی دہلی پر یورش کی" (p. 824).
- Akbar's second enthronement after Humayun's death: "دوبارہ شہنشاہ حسن تخت نشینی[OCR?] کلاہور میں مسعقد ہوا" (garbled) (p. 648).
- "اکبر بادشاہ[OCR?]" turned to Lahore's settlement from the start of his reign, built a city and a "دولت خانہ", raised large buildings and stayed for a time (p. 431). Lahore is called the capital in Akbar's time: Malik Ali was kotwal in "عہد اکبر بادشاہ" and held "تمام کوتوالی ... دارالسلطنتہ لاہور" (p. 194; surrounding words heavily flagged: "تمر[OCR?] قرویہ[OCR?] حتماً[OCR?] طلوکہ[OCR?] ... مقلم[OCR?]"); also "ملک علی کوتوال لاہور[OCR?]" (p. 193). Akbar is said to have been in Lahore when the Gilani ancestor came: "ان ایام میں اکبر بادشاہ … لاہو یہ رہا[OCR?]" (heavily damaged) (p. 525).
- Khwaja Ayaz (Nur Jahan's father), his wife and the infant travelled with a caravan "اونکی ساتہہ وہ لاہور تک پہنچی"; there he took service and rose to "میر منشی" under Akbar (p. 827).
- Coin legends of "نور الدین جہانگیر ابن اکبر بادشاہ": the transcriber's summary of four illegible lines names mints including "لاہور [OCR?]" and "احمد آباد [OCR?]" (p. 828).
- After Mahabat Khan's victory, "نورجہان لاہور کیطرف بہاگ گئی" (p. 829). Jahangir, ill on his way back from Kashmir, came to Lahore and died there ("جب وہان سے واپس ہوکر لاہور مین آئے تو ... خیمہ زن ملک عدم ہوگئے"); date as printed "تاریخ [illegible] ماہ نومبر سنہ ۱۶۲[illegible]ء [OCR?] مطابق سنہ [illegible]۳۶ [OCR?] ہجری" (p. 830). He was buried "شہر [OCR?] لاہور کے شمال رویہ لب دریای راوی موضع شاہدرہ مین" (p. 830); the tomb is "دریای راوی کی کنارے پر شہر لاہور سے بطرف شمال بفاصلہ چار میل" (p. 855), and from its minarets "تمام شہر لاہور معہ مسجد بادشاہی و وزیر خان وغیرہ عمارات نظر آتی ہین" (p. 860).
- "شاہجہاں بادشاہ لاہور میں ٹھہرے[OCR?]", in the anecdote of Haji Nur (p. 200). Shah Jahan is said to have had "بہت سی عمارات واقع لاہور" built, and Nawab Wazir Khan was appointed "ماظم[OCR?] لاہور" (p. 738); Wazir Khan is "ناظم لاہور" again in "جب نواب صاحب ناظم لاہور رستے[OCR?]" (p. 749). "لاہور مین شالامار باغ" is Shah Jahan's memorial: "چشم روزگار نے کوئی ایسا باغ کم دیکھا ہوگا" (p. 833).
- After Aurangzeb's accession, "دارا شکوہ نے لاہور کو مراجعت کی", raised a larger army there, then crossed the "دریای سندہ" (p. 836). Dara's betrayer, after handing him over in Delhi, "لاہور کیطرف چلا گیا" and was killed on the road (p. 838).
- At the start of Alamgir's reign Nawab Khalil Allah Khan held "دارہ[OCR?] لاہور" and, with a mansab of "شش ہزاری", was appointed "صوبہ دار لاہور" (p. 497).
- The great buildings stood until "عہد عالمگیر"; great amirs built "بڑی بڑی عمارات سنگین"; Abu al-Hasan son of Asaf Khan's buildings were among Lahore's chief ornaments (p. 431).
- In the succession war after Aurangzeb, Muhammad Mu'izz al-Din, governor of Multan, came with a large army and stores "بمقام لاہور" to his father Sultan Mu'azzam Bahadur Shah, and both went on to Akbarabad (p. 845). "شاہ عالم شہر لاہور مین سنہ ۱۷۱۲ء [OCR?] مین فوت ہوا" (p. 846).

**Later Mughal governors (nazims and subadars)**
- Abdus Samad Khan was given the title "سیف الدولہ دلیر جنگ بہادر" and appointed to Lahore by "شاہ عالم" (heavily flagged); he arrived when his son Zakariya Khan was "تیرہ سال کا" (p. 714). Zakariya Khan was appointed "ناظم لاہور" through the Sayyid brothers' support, Abdus Samad Khan moving to "ناظم ملتان"; later he continues as "ناظم لاہور" under Muhammad Shah, "شوق عمارت انکو معہ[OCR?] کمال تہا" (p. 716).
- In the reign of "محمد شاہ بادشاہ" a "حاکم[OCR?] لاہور" is named in connection with "مال گزاری[OCR?]"; the rest is damaged (p. 372).
- Mir Momin Khan became "ناظم[OCR?]" of Lahore in the time of "محمد شاہ[OCR?]" (p. 172); administration of the subjects was good, "رعایا ہی لاہور اوست بند ولسیت[OCR?] بہت عن[OCR?]"; he was not autonomous ("اگرچہ یہ نخود مختار نہ سے تھے"), "نبج[OCR?] باں[OCR?] خان" being "اصلی ناظم لاہور"; most buildings between Mochi Gate and Kashmiri Bazaar are his memorials, "دروازہ موچی و جنوب باروبہ[OCR?] بازار کشمیری اکثر عمارات … یادگار انکی"; and "یہ آخری ناظم لاہور ہوے" — after him no nazim was appointed or came (p. 173). Set beside this, without reconciliation: Nawab Abdullah Khan appointed "ناظم لاہور" in "سنہ ۱۱۶۱[OCR?]" (p. 717); the "حاکم لاہور" of p. 579; "خواجہ عابد صوبہ دار لاہور" (p. 578).
- Mir Mannu "بعہد محمد شاہ یہ نواب لاہور میں حاکم تھا"; the Sikhs had grown in number, and there was a general order to seize and kill them (p. 121). A Panjabi saying about him is illegible: "میر منو ساڈی[OCR?] [illegible] اسی[OCR?]" (p. 121).
- "بعدہ سنہ ۱۱۵۵[OCR?] میں صوبہ ملتان لاہور سکو شامل ہوا" (pp. 717–718).
- Under Nawab Abdullah Khan, "ناظم لاہور" from "سنہ ۱۱۶۱[OCR?]": "لاہور میں قحط پڑا", and he squeezed the people; proverb: "حکومت نواب عبداللہ[OCR?] نہ چکی[OCR?] رہی نہ چلہا[OCR?]" (p. 717).
- "خواجہ عابد صوبہ دار لاہور" attacked Charat Singh's garhi in "سن سترہ سو بیو کے ستہ[OCR?]"; "سکھوں کو فتح نصیب ہوئی" (p. 578).
- A Nawab ("نواب عارض[OCR?]", name damaged) came "کابل سے لاہور میں" (p. 722).
- The "حاکم اعلیٰ لاہور" is named as an office in Rajab Ali Khan's career (p. 141).

**Nadir Shah and Ahmad Shah Abdali**
- "نادر شاہ نی لاہور کو خوب لوٹا", after Shahnawaz Khan's defeat; he took cash and goods from Begumpura; Shahnawaz Khan's petitions to Delhi went unanswered (p. 717).
- At Ahmad Shah Abdali's assault, "شہر کو قتل کرکو اکثر مکانات کو جلا[OCR?] دیا[OCR?]" (p. 88). "کہتے ہیں کہ لاہور اونکی مہربانی سے احمد شاہ ابدالی سے [illegible] محفوظ رہا" — said of a majzub faqir buried at a khanqah (p. 91).
- Ahmad Shah Abdali came to Lahore ("احمد شاہ ابدالی [illegible] ... لاہور [illegible] آیا"); Shah Kaku Chishti told him "یہ میرا شہر ہی" and was sent as envoy (وکیل); the man he went to had him killed although "وکیل کو مارنا ممنوع ہی"; Ahmad Shah "غم وغصہ میں ڈوب کر دریای راوی[OCR?] ہی عبور کیا اور حکم قتل عام [illegible]" (p. 218). "ہزار ہا[OCR?] فوت ہو گئی"; he watched "نخاس[OCR?] کے دروازہ پہ بیٹھ کر"; Shah Nawaz Khan answered "اگر تاجر ہی تو بیچ ڈال اور اگر ظالم ہی تو قید کر دی اور اگر بادشاہ ہی تو معاف کر", was spared, and Ahmad Shah "تاج بخشی کرکے روانہ ہندوستان ہوا" (p. 219).
- Ahmad Shah came a second time and ordered a "قتل عام" of Lahore; "لاکہا آدمی" were killed and Begumpura looted (heavily damaged) (p. 718). Hafiz Lutfullah lived until "نواب ذکریا خان[OCR?]"; then "احمد شاہ ابدالی[OCR?]" carried out a "قتل" in Lahore and Begumpura was plundered; when Ahmad Shah came, the family moved into the city ("شہر میں چلے آتے تھے") and back to Baghbanpura when he left (p. 724).
- "جب تفرقہ عظیم[OCR?] سلطنت[OCR?] [illegible] میں پڑا اور میر[OCR?] مسمی[OCR?] احمد شاہ ابدالی [illegible] ہوا اور سکھوں[OCR?] لاہور کو [illegible] اور لوٹنا شروع کیا" (p. 372).
- Under Alamgir II, "افغانون نے پنجاب پر حملہ کرکے ملتان اور لاہور اپنی قبضہ مین کرلیا" (p. 851). Ahmad Shah Abdali had given the government of Lahore to "ایک عورت جو قوم سے پٹہانی تہی"; Ghazi al-Din arrested her, and Abdali invaded a third time (sentence breaks off at chunk end) (p. 851).
- The Marathas, "سکہون سے مدد لیکر", are said to have conquered "دہلی اور آگرہ اور ملتان اور لاہور" and driven the Afghans across "دریای اٹک"; no date given (p. 852).

**Sikh chiefs and Ranjit Singh**
- After Mir Mannu's death: "لاہور میں کہر[OCR?] ملکہر[OCR?] حکومت ہوگئی۔ جہان کسی سکہہ[OCR?] نے موقع پایا حاکم ہو بیٹھا" (p. 719). In the time of Sikh persecution ("وقت ایذا رسانی سکھاں") hundreds of people's property in the city is said to have been lost (very damaged) (p. 722).
- The three rulers, as variously printed (kept side by side): "حبب[OCR?] با وا پرسوتم داس یہان آئی تو لاہور میں تین حاکم تھی ایک گوجر[OCR?] سنگہ دوسرا لہنا سنگہ تیسرا سوبہا[OCR?] سنگہ روسائی لاہور", arrival date only "سمنت ۱۸[OCR?][illegible]" (p. 251); "پیشتر [OCR?] لاہور کے تین [OCR?] حاکم تھے ایک گوجر سنگھ دوسرا [OCR?] سنگھ تیسرا [OCR?] سنگھ", second and third names not printed, the fort built by "گوجر سنگھ نامی حاکم لاہور" (p. 326); "جب لاہور میں ... تین حاکم مقرر ہوئے" (p. 721); "جب لاہور میں تین حاکم مقرر ہوئی" the village was resettled (p. 724). Also "گوجر سنگھ میں [illegible] شہر لاہور [illegible] حصہ لاہور کا حاکم تھا" (p. 111).
- Lehna Singh: "لہنا سنگہ مکی [OCR?] از سرداگان [OCR?] لاہوری" (p. 855), "لہنا سنگہ حاکم سابق" (p. 858). When "لہنا سنگہ حاکم" began destroying the monuments of former kings, the elders invited Ranjit Singh to "لاہور مین آکر قابض ہو" (p. 786).
- Shah Zaman "نے اجازت دی کہ تم اگر لاہور پر قبضہ کر لو"; with the ruler unnamed ("حاکم لاہور [illegible]"), Ranjit Singh "… لاہور پر قبضہ کیا" (p. 579). "بعد ازان مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ نے لاہور پر قبضہ کر لیا" (p. 721); the Nawab's descendants had scattered to Shahjahanabad and Kabul, one being brought back and made "وکیل حاضر باش دربار سرکار کابل" (p. 721).
- Mehr Muhkam story (heavily garbled): someone came to Lahore by "دروازہ بھائی" and "بھائی دروارہ[OCR?] کہول دیا"; "دس ہزار آدمی سوار و پیادہ کو لاہور میں داخل ہو کیا ہے" (p. 646); "حب مہارا حد کے سو فتح کیا" with a garbled phrase about the "قلعہ" (p. 646); the Maharaja took hold of "زور ریہت[OCR?]" and brought "تمام چو دریہ مالا[OCR?] و نمبرداران نواحی شہر" under his shadow (p. 647); closing line "وہی لاہور اسرار کرے[OCR?] صاحبان عالی شان" (garbled) (p. 648).
- Under Ranjit Singh it was proclaimed "شہر میں" that whoever brought out a ta'zia would lose his head ("اسکا [OCR?] سر [illegible] ہوگا") (p. 274).
- In Ranjit Singh's time, red, black and marble stones "گرد و نواح لاہور کے" were torn down (p. 296). The buildings of the earlier era survived to "عہد مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ"; with no guardian, people [illegible] them; when the cantonments ("چھاؤنی[OCR?] ہائے") of Ranjit Singh's armies were built, the bricks of old tombs and houses were pulled out: "ہزار ہا مقابر و گنبد و مساجد و باغات و حویلیہائے[OCR?] قدیمہ جو یادگار امرائے سلف تھیں گم گشتیں[OCR?]" (p. 431).
- Nura was appointed to the "تعمیر خندق لاہور" after gaining access to Ranjit Singh's sarkar (p. 423).
- A weapon "ساختہ احمد شاہ" is placed at "مقام لاہور با رہ و ری ملتع[OCR?] نوایب[OCR?] وزیر خان میں جہاں اب کاب گھر[OCR?] ہے" (p. 575). The first misl, "بھسکھو بکی[OCR?]", is "قوم جاٹ ضلع لاہور" with "دس ہزار سوار"; the third misl's sardar "مجا سکہ[OCR?]" became ruler of "صلاع[OCR?] شرقی لاہور" (p. 575).
- Part of a sardar's fourth share was confiscated "ہمراہ فوج سکھو نکو[OCR?] ہنگامہ لاہور میں" (p. 577). "مظفر خان … لاہور میں پکہ نشن[OCR?] مقرر ہوئی" after Multan (garbled) (p. 580).
- Khushal Singh received "لاہور و امرتسر میں حویلیاں اور باغ"; Dina Nath built "ایک بڑا شوالہ لاہور کی [illegible] کے پاس" (p. 583). Among the "امرا و رؤسائے لاہور" who visited Mai Bhagi: "راجہ دینا ناتہہ سرداران سندہانوالیہ اور راجہ دہیان سنگہ" (p. 790).
- The Faqir family was known in Lahore as "خاندان فقیر صاحبان اور حکیموں" (p. 584); "ہر ایک اولی والی لاہور" knows their nobility; "لاہور میں تمام لوگ بلکہ پنجاب" call their mohalla "قطب یونان[OCR?]" (p. 585).

**After Ranjit Singh: the court, the wars and annexation**
- "مہاراجہ کھڑک سنگھ سمت ۱۸۹۷[OCR?] بکرمی ... میں فوت ہوئی[OCR?] اور مہاراجہ شیر سنگھ بھی اسی سمت میں تخت نشین ہوئی"; Sher Singh came to Lahore after the deaths of "کھڑک سنگھ و نونہال سنگھ" (p. 723). Raja Dhian Singh summoned Sher Singh "سو لاہور میں … تاکہ وہ تخت نشین ہو"; he came and pitched camp near the "چھاونی فوج خالصہ" (p. 588).
- "اور سوچیت سنگھ لاہور میں مارے گئے" (p. 582). A disturbance: "جب[OCR?] لاہور میں فساد شمل[OCR?] ہوا یہ شیر سنگھ ہوا"; the Gilani household raised a flag so that "سکھ لوگ" would know it as a house of the sadat (p. 525).
- Sher Singh's killers were sent to oblivion "بعد محاصرہ قلعہ لاہور و معرکہ عظیم" (p. 589).
- The heads of Hira Singh and Pandit Jalla (two accounts, kept side by side): after they were killed on the bank of the Ravi on "عید قربان", their heads and bodies were seen hanging; "بازار اور [illegible] اور لاہور کی [illegible]" are named; for "کمئی[OCR?] دن" the heads stayed there (p. 590). In the afterword: the heads of Raja Hira Singh and Pandit Jalla were hung from a gutter-spout ("پرنالہ") opposite the haveli where Jawahar Singh was held; a Sikh then kicked Pandit Jalla's head through "شہر لاہور" for several days; "راقم الحروف نی بچشم خود دیکہا" (p. 868).
- After the defeat at "سبھراؤں[OCR?]" the troops fled, looting, and "داخل لاہور ہوئے" (p. 591). Under the 1846 treaty territory passed from "سرکار لاہور", and "حسب درخواست اہالیان ریاست لاہور" "ایک ریزیڈنٹ لاہور میں مقرر ہوا"; disorder continued (p. 591). Raja Lal[OCR?] [Singh] "لاہور سے خارج ہو کر آگرہ[OCR?] میں بھیجا گیا" (p. 591).
- "[illegible] دسمبر سنہ ۱۸۴۹[OCR?]ء کو دلیپ سنگھ لاہور سے روانہ ہوا" (p. 592).

**British Lahore**
